ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e219	We are now going to deal with the long-term plan, and I want to start by commending the government and the government for providing a long-term research and higher education plan. It's long-awaited, and a lot of researchers and principals are happy that we're now doing what many people have dreamed about. It was Kristin Halvorsen, Minister of Knowledge of the Stoltenberg Government, who introduced this as part of the research report in spring 2013. The size joined such a long-term plan, although the Right in an annotation form was questioning the utility of such a plan. The joy shown by many in the submission of the plan together with the State budget may also be due to the uncertainty associated with whether this government wanted such a plan, as it is also not mentioned in the government’s platform. Building good research environments requires long and systematic investment over time. The environments that we know today do very well in the world context are at least 15-20 years old. Most of them are older. And if you want to get the most out of our resources, it's a good idea to see as much in context as possible. It concerns investment in knowledge building, research infrastructure, the number of scholarship holders and places of study. This predictability must at the same time build up within the areas of activity identified in the Research Communication, which are even more concrete in the long-term plan. Seas, climate and environment, a good and efficient public sector developing better welfare services, technological investment and business research is what we find in this plan, and that is very good. The building of life sciences at the University of Oslo and the construction of the new Ocean Space Center in Trondheim are good examples of interplay between thematic initiatives and infrastructure. This is also good and gives the sector more predictability. Nevertheless: The working party believes that the long-term plan suffers from three major shortcomings. It is not concrete enough, it is deficient in education, and it ignores other important processes in the sector that are taking place and that we can read about daily in the newspapers, which are about structure and funding. The Labour Party is not in disrepute with the sector in its criticism, as expressed by the Council of State in Today's Business Today. Like us, the sector was satisfied with what was coming in the harvest, but demanded more. The University and Higher Education Council, at its hearing, said: ‘Recruitment recruitment plans are good, but we will stress that the need is greater’. Abelia said: ‘We support the two construction projects, but call for a larger plan for which construction projects should be prioritised in the longer term and how they should be completed.’ Researcher’s association expressed its opinion: ‘In order for the proposed plan to become an important tool for the education and research sector, it is necessary to address some important shortcomings.’ That's what came out in the hearing among many other recordings. Let me go into the three points against which the Workers' Party objects. Firstly, the long-term plan is not sufficiently concrete. It lacks mention of study places, despite the fact that a unanimous staging was crystal clear on such an order. It has no list of buildings that should be built in the coming years. Except for the two buildings that I have now mentioned, there is nothing. 500 new recruitment positions and 400 million. Infrastructural kr is announced in the period 2015-2018. But then I would like to remind you of the front page of the plan, where the years 2015-2024 are the plan to apply. What about other buildings in the sector? They are not mentioned. What about the rehabilitating needs of the sector, which the Riksrevision has addressed as a matter of its own in Stortinget? One third of the building stock is in poor condition, especially construction for which the Ministry of Knowledge is responsible. And in order for these buildings to continue to be used, a significant part of the space for action goes to the institutions to maintain these. In the case of recruitment positions: According to information provided by the Nursing Association during the hearing, a total of 500 new jobs are needed during this period to ensure research-based training for the important professional profession of nursing. The government has added up to 500 in total. Then it gets too thin. The working party believes that the plan would have improved if it had included a prioritisation of nationally important research infrastructure, as well as a plan on how to meet future knowledge needs both within and outside the sector. We also ask for more information on the status and further development of the state of higher education and research in Norway, such as Norway. Annual R & D work per 1 000 persons employed, share of the higher education population, R & D effort per capita, R & D effort related to mainland GDP, innovation in business and development in publications and citations. The other thing missing is more in higher education. I would like to remind you that the message is called the Long-term Research and Higher Education Plan. It is possible that the Workers' Party should understand that education is implicit in everything that is mentioned in the plan, as the Council of State has repeatedly insinuated, but in that case more than us have not understood that education is widely mentioned. Then I choose to quote NSO, Norwegian student organisation: ‘The plan provides research, while higher education seems forgotten.’ It is evenly above the feedback we have received. Research and higher education are closely linked. It is precisely a prerequisite for high quality education that these tasks are carried out in parallel. One of the reasons is of course that it is among the students we find tomorrow's researchers, but an equally important reason is that close links between research and education give students an understanding of how research is done and how it can be used. Ensuring that Norway has workers who are able to understand and put new knowledge into practice must be a key element in focusing on research and development. That task has been neglected by the government. This morning the Study Barometer was presented. The results there could have been better. There is little doubt that the quality of education in some institutions, both at universities and higher education institutions, could be better at all, and a broad and systematic approach is required to raise the quality of education. When such analysis and proposals for action are lacking in this plan, the overall plan is weakened. In addition, the Stortinget has decided that we should have a report on quality in higher education — a proposal for which the Right was the driver. Until now, the Council of State has not been willing to respond to such a message or not. At least this message does not satisfy the Stortinget’s request in the previous period. A third weakness in the notification has become even more topical since the Committee's submission. We are facing several mergers in the sector. Last week it became known that NTNU wants to merge with three colleges, and that in a type of model that has been unknown so far – namely, with satellites geographically quite far away from the major university. Many others are in the same situation. North Norway will probably be left without any colleges, only with two universities. How many colleges are left? What does that mean for research, for education? What should be the division of roles between these institutions? It's unclear. To achieve the goals we have in this field, how does this new structure fit in? None of this is affected in the plan. In addition, the Government has notified an assessment of a new financing scheme. The conclusion is to be presented in the spring, but not as important as how universities and colleges are to be financed, is affected here – initially naturally in and separately for a long-term plan, given that everything else is fixed. But there is a danger that this plan will quickly expire when so much happens in parallel. The majority in Stortinget – all except the government parties – believe that the value of the long-term plan is weakened by the fact that both structure and funding are unclear when Stortinget deals with the plan. Research and knowledge development is nothing without important researchers and other professionals. The Norwegian Academy is facing a generation change. It will provide challenges to fill gaps for skilled professionals, but it also provides an opportunity to solve problems that have occurred in the sector over time. I am thinking first and foremost of the high use of temporaryity. There is a struggle to recruit the best among young people in Norway. Good youth is longing for. With a high degree of temporaryity, the academy is not as attractive as you would think, and as we would like. Not least there are some capable women who opt out of the sector, although they are absolutely good enough to be able to make a long career there. But they choose away because the uncertainty and unforeseeability become too incompatible with a stable family life. This is therefore also an opportunity for targeted action on how to recruit more women into academia, not least into top-level research positions, where men are still in an overwhelming majority. Over 60 pst. of the students are women, only 25 pst. by the professors are women. But there are also other challenges to recruitment. The government has talked a lot about world-leading research, and there are some researchers coming to Norway. Good conditions for scholarship holders are one reason, good nature and good family life are perhaps other reasons. But many are retreating — why? What can we do to make more people longer? This plan should be better used to create an overall strategy on this very thing, so that we can build world-leading environments. In a research debate, it should always be remembered that we agree that greater demands must be made on the results of research efforts, but we must be careful that policy becomes too instrumental. Research must never become something that is expected to achieve concrete and short-term policy objectives. Knowledge has a self-esteem and thus the knowledge society has its own justification, and it is particularly important to ensure the freedom of fundamental research in the future. I take up the proposal of the Workers' Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e351	When I began to engage in policy seriously, it was based on a burning commitment to higher education and research. It was based on the certainty that the basis of our future well-being is that we have a good education system, and that we are able to stimulate researchers to give their utmost in the fight to find answers to unresolved questions – answers to questions of vital importance to our society and business, which will once again benefit new students in education. My commitment also involves my own experiences from my time as a researcher at the University of Oslo, in the United States and at SINTEF and as head of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade and at the Education and Research Environment at Simula. Knowledge is the oil of the future for Norway, said Erna Solberg in his first New Year's speech as Prime Minister. In order to ensure jobs and well-being in the future, Norway needs higher education and research of better quality than today. We need to put in place a strategy for investment and structure for tomorrow's knowledge society that responds to our overall national needs – not just today, but many years ahead. We need to ensure that the best skills are available to Norwegian social and work life, and then we need to have educational institutions of international format. We need to ensure that Norway succeeds internationally, not least in the new EU research and innovation programme, Horizon 2020. It will provide Norwegian environments with valuable access to knowledge and competence. Public spending needs to be strengthened in order to keep pace with developments in other countries. We also need to stimulate increased investment in research and innovation in business. More than today, public investment should be directed towards the research environments we know to lead internationally, while maintaining a good academic base. Thus, in response to this, the government has promoted a long-term research and higher education plan drawing on common research and higher education objectives and priorities for the first time in history from a 10-year perspective. The overall objectives of the plan are: strengthening our competitiveness and innovation capacity to address major societal challenges in developing excellence in professional environments We need more top-class professional environments to develop Norway into an innovative nation, which can ensure our welfare society even after the oil adventure is over. The government has the ambition to develop more world-leading professional environments, and then the question is simple: how are we going to get it? The answer is, of course, not as simple, but the Long-term plan provides some overall objectives and priorities, while containing concrete escalation plans and growth in appropriations for both higher education and research. The Government announces concrete actions during the period 2015-2018, such as: to establish many more recruitment positions to step up funding for research infrastructure to strengthen Norwegian participation in the EU Framework Programme for Research and Innovation The plan takes into account the government's objective of making research 3 pst. of GDP by 2030. To succeed, however, it is not enough to give more money – we also need clear goals for what we want to achieve and how we will succeed. When we join the government's proposal, that means we can expect a rise of 1.4 billion. equipment, recruitment positions and enhanced European research cooperation. In addition, it implies an obligation to increase research appropriations each year until we reach the objective that public expenditure is equal to 1 pst. GDP. In addition, important construction projects, new life sciences, pharmacy and chemistry are prioritised at the University of Oslo and upgrading the Marine Technology Centre, the Ocean Space Centre in Trondheim. Both of these buildings underline the commitment to higher education, research and development and are fully in line with the government's thematic fields of activity. The new construction in the Gaustadbeck Valley here in Oslo will contain much of what the University of Oslo wants to be world leader in the future. This will allow for a pooling of different research environments in life sciences. Life science is a form of science in which living organisms such as plants, microbes, animals and humans are studied in a broad sense with different scientific approaches derived from subjects such as biology, chemistry, physics, pharmacy, molecular biology, informatics, medicine, nutrition and odontology. According to the President of the University of Oslo, this initiative is an important field for the education of students, and it gathers much of the knowledge we need in society in the future. Such a building will also be important for business in the future, where we are talking about a knowledge-based bioeconomy, which will be much greener than today's Norwegian economy. There's reason to do some reflections. We have plenty of evaluations that point to the challenges of today's education and research system. In 2014, the Swedish Research Council received a report prepared by Swedish professors Mats Benner and Gunnar Öquist, pointing out that Norwegian research is too marked by mediocrity and lack of ambition. A lot of people might object, but I think that's reason to listen. In order to succeed in research policy, the research system consisting of political governance, the research funding part and the performing entities must all work in the same direction. The report by Benner and Öquist underlines the importance of declaring high ambitions on quality at all levels, the importance of open recruitment and career care and the importance of further developing strong institutions with strong academic leadership. The long-term plan responds to this. We have enough sample reports and evaluations to point out that Norwegian higher education is not of sufficient quality. The message is that we are disseminating resources for research and higher education for the thin side. We have too many small and vulnerable professional environments offering the same education. As a result, many small institutions put in place strategies to compete with each other instead of cooperating. In order to improve quality, the government has therefore started to go through the structure of the university and higher education sectors. The objectives and measures set out in the long-term plan provide a good basis for the necessary changes in the higher education sector. In January, the government received proposals for a new funding system for universities and colleges from an expert group. These proposals will play an important role in the government's work on the announced call for proposals on the structure of the university and higher education sectors. Together, the interventions and objectives set out in the Long-term Plan and the Structural Communication will contribute to enhancing the quality of higher education and research in Norway. When the opposition in this Chamber, led by the Workers' Party's Marianne Aasen, expresses disappointment at the government's long-term plan, I believe that she is bombing quite strongly. I believe the Labour Party has more reason to be disappointed by its own efforts under the Red-Green Government, where words seemed to be more important than action. The Workers' Party was allowed to sit at the rudder of the Ministry of Knowledge for eight years — during a period of strong growth in public consumption without a corresponding growth in investment in higher education and research. Rather, there were few concrete steps to improve the quality of higher education and research. I cannot see that this is a claim that the long-term plan does not involve concrete commitments. In fact, it has been a long time since a document on research and higher education has been presented in the United Kingdom that has been as concrete as this. The government determines that the investments will be 3 pst. of GDP by 2030. Such an objective had already been removed by the red-green government in 2008. In addition, concrete obligatory escalation plans are described on key inputs involving 500 new recruitment positions, 400 million. kr in increased appropriations for research infrastructure and an increase of 400 million. kr to incentive schemes to stimulate Norwegian participation in the EU. In the long-term plan, the government sees higher education and research closely linked. Clear common objectives and priorities have been expressed. The plan is a clear shift in the direction of value creation, quality and the major challenges we face. Both the Research Council and the Academics have called the plan a milestone, while Unio has referred to it as both ambitious and concrete. The point is that we should create predictability and stable framework conditions for the sector, while taking into account that the world can look different in ten years' time. Therefore, we cannot lock ourselves to specific instruments for such a long period of time, neither in research nor in higher education. We have such an infinitely good starting point for success in the knowledge policy, but we need to combine the forces and strengthen the quality. In Norway, there are already good conditions for success, but we have to dry down some sharp priorities. The government has high ambitions, and that is why we have also seen a change of pace in policy where a good school, skilled skilled workers and the quality of higher education and research are among the government's most important projects. Since Erna Solberg took over as Prime Minister in 2013, we have seen concrete action through strong budgets. Now we see that the government both invests more and makes concrete contributions to enhance the quality of our education and research environments. Faggot words are one thing, concrete action is another. That's what separates the clone from the wheat.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e479	When Bishop Johan Ernst Gunnerus together with Schøning and Suhm founded the Royal Norwegian Society of Sciences in Trondheim in 1760, a new era began in Norway. Before this, largely Norwegian research — or in the smallest research carried out by Norwegian professionals — had been carried out in our neighbouring country, which was a few years ahead of us in this field. The Royal Norwegian Society of Sciences created a revolution in which the academic career path was made known and accessible to more than theologians and a few other selected professional groups. When we got our first university, the University of Oslo, about 50 years later, after the establishment of DKNVS, we got the opportunity to connect academic education and research, as we do today. From the bishop and naturalist Gunnerus via e.g. The mathematician Abel, the chemist Onsager, the archaeologist Heyerdahl and until today there has been some development. Now, 250 years ago, while you've been arguing about Newton's three laws, today you're researching vital medicine, Alzheimer's, whether it's actually possible to print 3D a whole house. The situation is slightly different today, including in Norway. It's been a strength for our environments that we've been together in this room about the overall leadership, the problem description and the diagnosis, even though the medicine sometimes bears the mark of a little different solutions. The long-term plan is a milestone for research and higher education, which we have agreed to have. For the first time, a plan providing predictability and long-termity to education and research communities and businesses is presented. The government has defined three overall objectives for this plan: to strengthen competitiveness and innovation, to meet the major societal challenges and to develop excellent professional environments. In terms of competitiveness and innovativeness, it is a well-known matter that we will never – at least not in the near future – be the cheapest in Norway, but we can be the best. Both industry and the public sector depend on this. In a new economic everyday life without the oil and gas sector being the ultimate valuer, business needs to find new solutions and innovate products to maintain jobs and strengthen value creation. For the public, innovation is essential to meet an increasingly demanding market. Solving the major societal challenges is about being ahead of development. We know there's going to be an older wave. We know that we have – and most likely will – even greater challenges with security and preparedness in the future, and we know that access to food and drink worldwide will be challenging. Common to these is that they can be solved, just as they've been solved before. Research and development and innovation will be crucial for managing this together with the education system. What someone has criticized this plan for is that it is so-called one-sidedly concerned with world-leading environments and excellent quality. Of course, that's not true, but it has a completely deserved place in the plan. As I mentioned at the beginning of the article, we largely agree on the diagnosis of research and higher education across the blocks. Part of this diagnosis is clear that we have too many small and too many fragmented environments that provide research and education at an unsatisfactory level. High quality has to be a continuous measure. If we are to attract the best professionals, including foreign workers, and prevent too many of our own from moving to more exciting environments abroad, this is vital. The government announced that it would step up funding for research and higher education within six long-term priorities. It is also announced that R & D will increase the appropriations for R & D up to 1 pst. of gross domestic product and that, with today’s outlook, it will be reached around 2020. In addition, the objective of 3 pst is introduced. GDP should go to R & D by 2030. It was a goal that the previous large-scale majority removed, and I am happy to record that they are now regretting it. To get to all of this and to make sure that it's not just nice numbers and happy parties, it has to be made concrete. How are we going to achieve these objectives? Yes, by increasing recruitment positions by 500, by increasing the allocation to research infrastructure by 400 million. and by multiplying the appropriations to ensure high and good participation in Horizon 2020. We're on it. The 2015 government budget follows the long-term plan by around 800 million. kr. The real growth rate in research and development appropriations is 4.5 pst. at the top of a good R & D year in 2014. Never before have we been closer to the 1 pst. goal, and even closer we're going to get in the years that come. One of the main actions launched in the plan is a strong and absolutely necessary stepping up of the STIM-EU and other incentives to increase the return share from the world’s largest research competition and cooperation, Horizon 2020. In order to achieve that ambition, we have to increase participation by over 60 pst. compared to the 7th Framework Programme. Then we must have leading environments that compete with the very best, and where the environment must have conditions that enable us to participate in the EU arena. Unfortunately, the last element has not been the case for the research institutes until now. SINTEF and the Norwegian Research Council have estimated that the incentive framework needs to be increased by 350 million. kr to achieve the objective of Norwegian participation in Horizon 2020. Fasit in the plan is 400 million. kr. The plan points to two particularly important buildings in the period that will be prioritised, namely the Life Science Centre in Oslo and the Ocean Space Centre in Trondheim. These are two very important buildings, which many people have talked about, but with which something is finally happening. These buildings are fully in line with the objectives set out in the plan, and will be very important in order for the escalation and long-term sustainability to be realised. The plan has received criticism from the opposition to be too thin. It's a special form of criticism. The red-greens presented a research report in 2013, which was longer than this action plan, but it did not contain a single concrete escalation. We are able to achieve very clear priorities and targets for the sector on just under 50 pages. It's a force, not a weakness. In particular, the criticism has come that higher education is devoted too little space for research. It's a shame that the opposition can't see these more in context than what you're actually doing. Research and higher education are in most respects inextricably linked — including in this plan. When we say we're betting on world-leading environments, it's about both research and higher education. When we focus on enabling technologies, it's about both research and higher education. When we implement a historical effort on the number of recruitment positions, it affects both research and higher education. This is a historic day. It is an ambitious and good plan that will be adopted. Finally, the sector shall have the long-termity and predictability it deserves. Now you can finally start looking forward. The knowledge society is on its way.

We know that there is a highly skilled workforce to drive our society through these transformations, and we know that probably new knowledge and new solutions are some of the most important tools. ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e992	In the long-term plan, we can read that, in addition to the ongoing investment in quality in research and higher education, government prioritises its own investment in world-leading trade unions. ‘Knowledge and competence are among the most important competitive factors we have. ... good people with good skills are the starting point for how we meet major societal challenges. This is also what facilitates value creation, both in the public sector and in business. Research and education affect the economy by increasing the quality of labour and services provided, and by enabling new solutions and products to be developed and put into practice. This contributes to the ability to convert and to increase productivity. A knowledge-based approach is essential for finding solutions that can meet many of the challenges facing society.’ These are benevolent words and difficult for some to disagree with. Now I'm not going to talk about all the shortcomings in the long-term plan, there have been several things that have been done before me, but there's nothing to do with the problem of temporary employees in the sector, and I find that to be alarming. For how are we going to get young people to choose to become researchers, how are we going to get the best, those with the best assumptions, skills and abilities, for the sector? And not least: how are we going to get women to choose the profession when one of the biggest problems for recruitment to research is temporary jobs and that it is so widespread? For many years, many people have been talking about this, and the Research Association – and I stress: the Research Association, not LO! – writes, inter alia, this on its website: ‘There is an abnormally high temporaryity in sectors where the Research Association organises workers. This affects, in particular, scientific staff, but also other groups in the research sector, which are assigned much more temporarily than usual in the labour market. The general rule in Norwegian working life is permanent addition. It improves the safety and predictability of workers and provides a better basis for further professional development. Being temporarily employed can be burdensome in several ways. Most seriously, there is enough uncertainty about the loss of jobs and income, but temporary employees may also feel that they are being neglected in some job contexts, such as pay, job creation, access to resources and strategic work. It may also affect academic freedom and the inadequacy of research when the job situation of researchers is insecure. Furthermore, the bank will often suffer from poorer loan conditions and the loss of some financial benefits to permanent employees.’ Furthermore, they write that the benefits of fixed additions include: is that it ‘— providing the activities and individual opportunities for long-term and comprehensive planning — strengthening the working environment and well-being — providing mutual trust and loyalty — ensuring academic freedom and thus the trust of society in critical and independent research — making the workplace more attractive to the talents that will not be missed — providing the opportunity to recruit the best available competence in an addition process — reducing the number of addition processes and periods of training — preventing brainpower from disappearing and shifting focus from key job search tasks’ The figures for employees in the university and higher education sectors from the Databank on Right-wing Education in December 2013 show that women are assigned far more than men in temporary posts. The team of researchers comments on it in this way: ‘The mid-term of the sector is a significant problem for all involved, but this shows that women are assigned more than men on a temporary basis and indicates an additional challenge for gender equality and working conditions.’ That is why this matter is important for the Research Association. On VG Net right now we can read about Sanna Sarromaa in 35 years, which is now giving up the academy. She has been temporarily employed for ten years, but now she can't do more, and it says well. In the past, I have understood the Council of State that he believes that this problem must be taken seriously, but now I am not so sure what he means anymore. We know that the government wants more temporary work, and they want to change the Working Environment Act to get this done. Neither the Right Party, the Advance Party, the Left Party or the Christian People's Party support our proposal in this matter, where, together with the Centre Party and the Swedish Government, we ask the government to establish a recruitment strategy for better gender balance and to ensure less use of temporaryity in the university and higher education sectors. When they are likely to vote against this proposal later today, it must mean that they are actually opposed to what is in our proposal, as well. I think it is serious that they do not take this seriously. Then it is to be admitted that it could have happened more during the previous government, even though there was a sharp decline from 19.7 p.m. from 2005 to 14.8 pst. in 2012. For the Workers' Party, it is important that the work of the Red-Greens is now continued to ensure that one not only manages to recruit good researchers, but actually manages to keep them in the sector. Finally, it's a paradox that while in this case everyone stresses the importance of research and knowledge for our society, the government chooses to ignore established knowledge when it comes to temporaryity.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e1233	I refer to the proposal by the representatives of Anne Tingelstad Wøien, Kjersti Toppe, Jenny Følling and Heidi Greni to lift the requirement of a continuous withdrawal of the right to secondary education. The Committee on Churches, Education and Research has examined the proposal, and I am presenting the Committee's opinion today. I would first of all like to thank an overall committee for good cooperation and refer to the joint comments of the committee. Young people who have completed primary or equivalent training are entitled to three years of secondary education upon application. This right must normally be taken out during a continuous period of five years, or six years when the training is provided in full or in part in an apprenticeship. The right must be exercised by the end of the year when the student reaches the age of 24. Adults who have not completed secondary education are entitled to secondary education as of the year of age 25. In other words, there is a distinction between the right to secondary education under youth law and under adult law. Youth who do not complete secondary education during a continuous period of five or six years has exercised the right of youth. If you are under 25 then you have to wait until your age of 25, before you have a statutory right to further education. An overall committee agrees that today’s system can be a challenge for those who are going between two chairs. There is also reason to stress that, in a joint comment, the Committee refers to the general obligation of the county to offer offers to applicants without the right to secondary education, but that in such cases it will be up to the county council to decide on the application. In addition, young people who have exercised juvenile rights — if there are particular reasons — may apply for secondary education under adult law, such as: because, for health reasons, it is not possible to make use of youth law. Nevertheless, the Committee acknowledges that today’s system is leading to a risk of some being left untrained for a period of time and that there is a risk of losing the motivation to complete secondary education. In view of the close link between the implementation of secondary education and subsequent labour relations, the Committee is united on the intention to facilitate the widest possible implementation of secondary education and on the need for more flexibility than the present system provides. However, the Committee shall submit a split opinion. I leave to the speakers who come after me to explain their views and, in the course of this discussion, will explain the Right's view on the matter and why, in majority, together with the Christian People's Party and the Progress Party, we have concluded with a recommendation that the proposal for a representative be annexed to the Minutes. The majority do not want to take an hasty decision to make a legislative change as set out in the draft representative. It is necessary to carefully investigate any legislative changes. Moreover, it has not been given that the amendment of the Training Act in the manner proposed in this case is neither the only nor the best solution to ensure that those currently serving between two chairs will have the opportunity to complete their training. The whole Committee shares the views of the proposers on the need for greater flexibility in order to ensure that all those who have not completed secondary education are able to implement without prejudice to the system. Because of this, the Government Platform states that the government will introduce a ‘lifelong right to secondary education’, and in its work on the Lifelong Learning and Exclusion Communication, the introduction of a seamless transition between youth law and adult law will be assessed. The notified notification has been decisive for the majority’s conclusion in this case. We do not want to agree to a change in the law until it has been thoroughly investigated, nor is it necessary to ask the government to make such a change in the law because this will be assessed in that notice, and then the Stortinge will have the opportunity to deal with it carefully. I look forward to a good debate.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e1376	Youth who have completed primary or equivalent training is entitled to 3 years of secondary education. The Court must be removed during a continuous period of 5 years, or 6 years when the training is provided in full or in part in an apprenticeship and by the end of the year in which he reaches the age of 24. Adults, for their part, are entitled to secondary education under certain conditions from the year they reach the age of 25. So we're distinguishing between the right to secondary education under youth law and the right to adult education. The reason for this is that the two groups have different needs. Adults over 25 years of age are a more complex and heterogeneous group than young people, and training should therefore be adapted to the individual's assumptions and circumstances more closely than in the case of young people. For example, an adult can take all or part of a subject, be offered evening lessons, distance learning and the possibility to take training in a shorter or longer time. Secondary education under juvenile law is mainly provided as a common day-to-day education. With today’s legislation, it may be a challenge for young adults who do not start or complete secondary education in the context of youth law that adult law does not take place until they reach the age of 25 years – as the representative proposal poses a problem. We know that some people who, for various reasons, have failed to complete the course of juvenile education, but who are also not 25 years old, are going to go and wait for a new opportunity in adult law. Such waiting is unfortunate and unnecessary. In practice, however, there are solutions for this group, and there is reason to believe that many of those who fall between youth and adult law actually receive an offer of secondary education. Firstly, it is possible to apply for secondary education even if the formal conditions are not met, but it is then up to the county to decide whether the application is granted. Secondly, young people who are initially entitled to secondary education under the law of their youth but who have problems taking up the full court in time may apply for secondary education under adult law. A condition for this is that there are particular reasons. This could be done, for example, by: are health reasons for which the person concerned has not taken up the full court within the age of 24 years. Although there are practical solutions for many in this group, I agree with the proposers that today’s system may lead to some people being at risk of being without a training offer while waiting to apply for training under adult law. There is a risk that they will lose the motivation to complete secondary education. I also agree with the proposers that higher education is the key to higher education and participation in the labour market. Research shows that the implementation of secondary education is a key to participation in the labour market. Labour skills requirements are also increasing. The labour projections of the Central Statistical Office up to 2030 show that the demand for labour with only primary or only partially completed secondary education is likely to decline. That is why I believe it is important that the law facilitates as much as possible completion and completion of secondary education. The government is developing a new and comprehensive policy for adults who are at risk of falling out of work and who are out of work. This work will be presented in a Communication to the EEA on Lifelong Learning and Exclusion during 2015. The notification shall be drawn up jointly with the Ministry of Knowledge, the Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs and the Ministry of Children, Gender and Inclusion. In the government platform, we have determined that the government will give lifelong entitlement to higher education. As part of this, we consider in the context of the communication to the Stortinget to introduce a seamless transition between youth law and adult law. It will ensure that everyone who has completed primary school, regardless of age, is entitled to secondary education at the time they themselves wish to take the training. I would like to stress that it is important to carefully assess possible legislative changes. There are also other ways to address the challenge of young people who do not reach the age of 24 years through the exercise of full juvenile rights, than to lift the requirement of a 5-year continuous withdrawal of juvenile law. One possibility is, for example: making it easier to choose to take out the right to secondary education under adult law even if you have not reached the age of 25. I share the views of the proposers on the need for greater flexibility, but I would advise against an accelerated legislative change now – precisely because of the ongoing work on the communication to the Stortningen on lifelong learning and exclusion. In this work, the most appropriate mechanism for a good relationship between youth law and adult law will be assessed, and the proposal for a representative will also be considered in this context.

However, I would like to begin by thanking the Committee for a good cooperation in this matter and the representatives for raising an important topic, although the conclusions are somewhat different between the parties. The main points of disagreement within the Committee were the proposal to convert the national samples into sample samples. I would therefore like to use most of my contribution to explain the views of the majority of the committee in this matter, not least because I believe that the proposers and the minority will explain their views well later in the debate. The national tests were introduced as part of the Knowledge Commitment in 2004 and in response to the fact that at the end of the 1990s and early 2000s international studies both revealed that Norwegian school was not good enough to learn away and that we had far too little knowledge of how the schools were doing. We also knew too little about the differences between the performance of municipalities and how each school delivered. That's why you needed a tool -- something that could show you how to better map your knowledge. We simply needed knowledge about the school in order to get more knowledge of the schools. So these tests have been altered several times along the way, and a large majority have defended the tests. Last autumn, national tests were carried out on 360,000 students. The 5th and the 8th steps had them in English, reading and billing, and the 9th steps had them in reading and billing. Therefore, I am somewhat surprised that the Centre Party and SV will now remove this tool and replace it with sample samples. This is a tool like 65 pst. The Norwegian schoolleaders say that it is important for teachers to be able to develop school and education, and that it is 85 pst. The school owners, i.e. the municipalities and the counties, report that an important and good tool for developing the school locally. Then I have to say, with all due respect for the proposers and the minority supporting the proposals, that I have problems understanding what makes education policymakers propose to deprive the school of such a tool as it itself states is important in order to gain more knowledge into classrooms. Samples will be quite different from national samples and a much lower tool for schools. It may be typically socialist to think that you can help each student by calculating the average, but it doesn't believe the majority. If you're going to help each one, we're going to have to help each one. The national samples have been renewed and improved. Proposal No. 2 from the representatives are that you have to get a better way of presenting the samples. Here I believe that the Ministry of Knowledge has been in front of the representatives and has already done so. With four very simple grips you have now got a new and more precise scale. The development of schools and municipalities is now being measured, not just annual results. Teachers are given a new digital tool that is easier to use and less bureaucratic to use, and there is no provision for ranking between schools. This means that the National Samples tool is something that we have constantly renewed and improved. But there is a big difference between that and doing as the proposers suggest, namely removing the tests. A third proposal from the proposers is to reduce overall school reporting requirements. Here I experience a broad – yes, in fact unanimous – agreement in Norwegian policy that it is an important part of school policy. But in this context, I just have to say that a time thief is not everything that takes time. We do things in school that take time, and it's important that we do because it monitors and provides us with documentation about the students' learning. The students are entitled to that. We should keep doing that. A fourth proposal in the draft document by the representatives is to deprive the municipalities of the possibility of setting local requirements for monitoring and documentation. This is seen by the majority as a very clear violation of the school policy we have pursued in Norway every year, namely that local school owners, municipalities and counties are responsible for developing knowledge policies in their community. I have to say that the sum of first to deprive the school owners of it as 85 pst. of the municipalities that is an important tool for providing more learning in the classroom, and then refusing to introduce other tools to monitor knowledge, provides a proposal that is, to put it mildly, surprising. That it comes from the Center Party, a party that normally fights for local self-government, is even more surprising. With that, I want a good debate.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e1474	The mapping of skills is important in order to be able to target training so that learning actually takes place, and educators and teachers have been surveyed every year in order to adapt education to the best of students. It is a method that is widely agreed to have an effect, and fortunately, the proposal for a representative we are discussing today does not intend to do so. But although there is a broad consensus on mapping as a method, it is timely to discuss which mapping has the best effect. We have to ask ourselves, what is the goal of the mapping? I believe that assessment for learning should be a general principle for testing students in Norwegian school. Monitoring should therefore have a purpose in addition to being only a report and control of school systems. As the Advocate General pointed out, the national tests have been amended several times during their lifetime for the better. These include: develop guidance materials to ensure that the results are not only a system-wide reporting but are used for educational development at school and student level. But it's probably also right to say that in many places this aspect gets a little bit in the shadow. It is confirmed by the evaluation of national samples carried out in 2013. In this evaluation, the teachers say that the desired effect of the tests disappears because they do not have time to enter the results of each individual student in a way that promotes learning in the classroom. This finding underlines the need to see the time use and monitoring system in the context. I often feel that the frustration surrounding local monitoring is greater than the frustration surrounding the national level. In the Training Act, we have said that municipalities must have a sound system to assess whether the requirements of the Training Act are being complied with and that a quality report is to be provided for discussion on an annual basis in the Municipal Council. There's a tendency for this to boil down to numbers, graphs and get the best possible results on the table. I do not believe that local monitoring should be banned, but it may be necessary to discuss stronger leadership in the scope of local monitoring. Is it perhaps the case that with PISA, TIMSS, PIRLS, national samples, mandatory screening tests and local screening we carry out some unnecessary double screening? With this as a hindrance, the Labour Party has proposed in the vote on the proposal for a representative that the government will come back with a case in which the overall monitoring system in the school is reviewed. The case shall include an assessment of whether today’s system is structured in a way that ensures effective time use and reduces bureaucracy, while ensuring national and local school development for the benefit of students’ learning. We're the ones who are the leaders in how the test systems are perceived and used in school. Changes in samples from 2014, such as: making it easier to measure changes over time is initially positive, but I am highly sceptical about the changes related to publication. This leads to a ranking focus at system level and not primarily in classroom learning. The focus is shifted from Petter in 10 years and his reading skills to a graph and just an average. That is not a good assessment practice. The time spent by teachers and the reduction of unnecessary bureaucracy are the starting point of the proposal for a representative. Here we share the concerns of the proposers. We therefore want to vote in favour of points 2 and 3 of the proposal, which aim to make the tests more effective and targeted, and that there is a high pressure to reduce the total school bureaucracy. And just to underline it, the last thing is about a lot, a lot more than just mapping. With this, I am promoting the Labour Party's proposal in the matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e1492	The school is one of the greatest and most important welfare goods in our society. Here, children and young people lay the foundation for their future. The main purpose of the school is to contribute to the learning of children and young people and to facilitate students to acquire knowledge and skills and become independent individuals. National screening tests are one of the tools for teachers, school leaders, school owners and parents to obtain information on the success of the school in developing their skills. Norwegian students perform roughly on average in the OECD area, and one third of the students do not complete secondary education. We're simply not good enough to catch up and follow up students who have learning difficulties or who need extra challenges. National tests provide information on the student, school, municipality or county council needs additional follow-up in order to achieve the desired results. 86 pst. In the NCI evaluation of national tests for 2014, the school owners say that the results are important for the development of schools in municipalities. School owners and principals have an important responsibility, and that they have knowledge of the results of the schools is a prerequisite for further development of the school. Representatives ask the government to ensure that the overall reporting and documentation requirements in schools are reduced. The Progress Party supports the intention to reduce bureaucracy in school, and believes it is important that teachers spend as much of their time as possible on students. It is therefore gratifying that the Right Party Government is already reducing red tape in school. The Minister of Knowledge received just before Christmas last year’s report ‘Reporting and documentation requirements in the school sector’. It has been developed in cooperation between the KS and the Ministry of Knowledge. The government has been clear that the work on reducing red tape in school will be a priority. The KS and the Ministry will follow up the main objective of the project, which is to reduce the overall level of documentation and reporting requirements in School-Norway. This shall apply to both national and municipal requirements. However, it is particularly true that the Centre Party proposes that no local monitoring requirements can be imposed. In practice, the proposal involves prohibiting active school ownership at local level and will weaken local authorities’ capacity to act. The proposal implies a new model for the national samples, where the samples are carried out as sample samples. The party is concerned that school owners and school leaders should use the test results to improve the performance of students at school. The primary purpose of the national tests is that the results are used to further develop the students. With this in mind, the Progress Party believes that the model proposed by the representatives here has some significant weaknesses. Samples will only produce results at national level, which we already receive information about through international studies. If only a sample of the students carry out the tests, teachers will not be informed of individual liver outcomes. Thus, the proposal undermines some of the purpose of the national tests. With the model proposed here, teachers will not be able to use national tests for follow-up and pedagogical development, and school owners will not have the necessary management information about the students’ competence in reading, Norwegian and English. Furthermore, it will be more difficult to identify municipalities with particularly strong or weak results, making it difficult to identify municipalities that need extra follow-up or that have a good school that they can share with others. The representatives ask the government to provide a presentation of the results, because the national tests provide more precise and correct information than as of October 2014, when this proposal for a representative was promoted. Recent changes in the presentation of the national samples have been made by the right-hand Party Government and, from December 2014, the results of the tests were presented on a new scale allowing for the measurement of change over time. This means that national tests are now an even more important and better management tool for school leaders and school owners. The party to the progress is satisfied that the presentation of the results of the national tests has improved and changed and that the government is already reducing the overall level of school reporting and documentation requirements.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e1518	Testing in school is something that takes a lot of people. Some consider tests to be important management tools that are essential for the development of good school provision, while others consider the testing of students as unnecessary bureaucratic time thieves who steal attention away from learning and who weaken education rather than strengthen it. So it might be possible to have a certain understanding of both views. If there are too many demands for testing, measuring and reporting in school, it can be felt as a burden, both for teachers and students, and there may be a risk that when you first start testing one thing, you get very concerned about exactly what you've decided to test. The father knows that we're getting a little too narrow a perspective on what's going on in school. The danger is that you get so concerned about getting the best results from the different tests that you lose sight of other important aspects of learning. We are at risk of going into the trap that Arve Tellefsen warned against earlier this week, that we are making the students «calculates». We don't have to go into the trap, because what our society needs is no more calculators, but whole people. But at the same time, we know that mapping and testing also have value. National tests are an important tool for teachers, school leaders and school owners. It is important to have information about results in order to develop the school further. It makes it possible to find out what works, what does not work, at school, and it makes it possible to share ‘best practice’ and it makes it possible to deploy resources and measures where necessary. And I think that if these samples are converted into samples, we will lose a lot of value at local level. So at least we should not do this without a thorough analysis of the consequences and possibly a survey of the need for supplementary tests, adapted to school and municipality levels. So it's important that the tests are designed and conducted in a way that doesn't require too much time from teachers and students, and that both the design and presentation of the results are done in such a way that they provide the best possible benefits for teachers, school leaders and school owners. So I think it's important that these tests and surveys do not contribute too much to creating a competitive society, where the students are constantly participants in a competition to make it the best possible. It won't be a good thing for learning, it won't be a good thing for social development, and it won't be a good thing for the community, which you have to establish in order for the students to have a good learning environment. Then I have a certain understanding that the proposers will prevent local monitoring and documentation requirements from being imposed. I assume that the background to this proposal is some cases where the testing and reporting regime has taken a little off, and we know more examples of that. But I still think it is unreasonable to introduce such a provision, because it will prevent municipalities from carrying out surveys that may be appropriate to address local issues. I think we need the national tests. They are an important tool for the development of the school, but at the same time we need to make sure that the test regime does not grow too large, that the tests do not narrow our field of vision, and that school bureaucracy does not become too overwhelming. I therefore hope that cooperation between the Department and the KS to reduce reporting and documentation in school will bear fruit.

But there has to be a match between the time and resources spent by teachers and students on these tests and the gain it brings. In particular, we believe that there are three reasons why samples should be taken as sample samples. First, sample samples will reduce the pressure to set aside time in normal hours to practice the tests. So there's going to be more time for the teacher to do real teaching for all the students. Secondly, it would make it less interesting to rank schools in the media, and thirdly, it would save resources, both for those who are going to be tested in the bureaucracy, and for teachers. In our opinion, it is about to go into a parody, and that is why we believe that the municipalities should not be able to introduce even more tests than what the authorities are already offering today. It impresses us when parents and teachers tell us how target management and test regimens affect teaching, and not least affect students. One of these testimonies comes from a mother who sails is a teacher, named Gunhild Nohre-Walldén, in a chronicle for a while back. There she writes how as a mother she experiences what she describes like this: ‘The manic use of testing and monitoring by the Oslo School reflects a sad and tragic view of learning and assessment.’ When her then fifth grade was assessed according to a total of 127 concrete learning goals throughout the school year and during the 1st to 7th year. class shall pass through approx. 350 tests, it gives us a clear picture that education policy-makers have to take seriously. Making national samples as samples is not exactly a revolution, but it is a step in one direction. Today I know that the classes practice national tests in advance to be good at solving the problems when they get them. Does it give credibility to a test that students actually can – ‘teach for test’? Not in our eyes. The question of national tests is only one element of a test regime in the process of running. Not only does it steal time from teaching and snuggle the teaching, it seduces both politicians, school leaders and parents, and most seriously: it causes some students to lose motivation and ‘door’ a little every day.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e1580	The school was the theme when President Barack Obama gave his annual State of the Union speech three years ago. Obama was concerned about developments in American school, where a huge focus on standardized tests, ranking and competition threatened the quality of the entire school system. And to quote Obama: «... grant schools flexibility: To teach with creativity and passion; to stop teaching to the test». Developments in Norway towards a test regime are not as strong, but we go in the same direction. That's why I want to commend the centre party for the proposal to reduce the amount of tests in school and make the national samples selection samples. This is completely in line with SV's program over many years. If we want to improve the quality of Norwegian school, put individual life needs in the centre and give teachers greater professional freedom and autonomy, this is one of the most important proposals we in the Education Committee are dealing with this year. SV asked the department at Stortinget to provide an overview of tests and surveys in primary schools in the 10 largest Norwegian municipalities. This list does not exist publicly today. It shows that, in addition to the national tests, the international studies participated in by Norway, PISA, TIMSS, PIRLS, ICCS and ICILS, the mandatory screening tests and the voluntary screening tests, a number of municipalities have introduced their own screening tests and systems, which are in addition to those imposed nationally. Let me take reading as an example. The drama stands out clearly with most mandatory tools in Norwegian reading with testing at all stages. Kristiansand and Tromsø have also introduced mandatory tests at several stages, in addition to the voluntary screening tests that are made mandatory. Fredrikstad has the ‘Kartleggerer’ in Norwegian, Kristiansand has the ‘letter test’, Oslo has the ‘Osloprobe’, Stavanger has the ‘Carlsten reading test’, Tromsø has the ‘Kartlegger in reading’. Similar own tests and mappings are introduced in mathematics and English. Teachers need tests to see how their students are doing. Teachers should therefore be free to design the tests they need to follow up their elephants. But the national tests do not provide such information. National tests are not designed to provide supportive feedback to individual students and to strengthen them in their learning work. They can't be used for it. And many of the municipal samples imposed are of exactly the same character. Teachers have not asked for them. The national tests also do not say anything about which schools do good learning work. They may at best say something about which schools have the best students, but they don't say anything about where the best teachers are. First of all, the national tests confirm the nature of the school's parental background. In a study carried out by the Education Association in January of this year, 71 pst respond. members of the management are associated with their opposition to the publication of national test results at school level. Also a clear multiple, 69 pst. of the school leaders asked, they agree in whole or in part that such publication will not increase the quality of school education. When the results of the national tests have become the currency of a school market in which schools compete against each other and are measured against each other, where the principals have principal contracts where better grades lead to higher pay, and where teachers tell that weak elephants are kept away from the tests, then national tests have become a barrier to good school development, not a tool. This test culture does something about the way we look at school. Let me take Oslo School as an example, the Right Exhibition Window. There is one that sees schools as knowledge companies producing results. Schools are performance units with measurable and unambiguous measurements. The City Council has 48 targets for Oslo School. The principal is responsible for achieving these goals, and the individual teacher is responsible for achieving the goals of their students. There are strong links between the results achieved, the set-aside and the sanctions. The control parameters are directed towards the student results, e.g. national tests. The rector's contracts, which are secret, have 86 performance targets. Good results are high, bad results are not. For SV, the school is not a factory to produce results. It's a community institution, based on a broad societal mandate and a broad knowledge view. The school shall be based on the needs and curiosity of each individual student. What teachers need is no longer overarching, but greater professional freedom. If we're going to close it down, the test press in the Norwegian school must be reduced. The test press is a driver of surface learning, the learning that helps you try tomorrow – not the learning that goes deep, that the whole community screams for, and that all the elephants need. I could imagine that state councillor Røe Isaksen once said the same thing as Obama: we give the schools and the teachers the freedom to learn away with creativity and commitment, to avoid spending valuable teaching time on ever-new tests. Learning should not be about the test, but about the student.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e1775	This is a Document 8 proposed by representatives of the Workers' Party to allow the use of lead hail outside wetlands and shooting paths. The committee has spent a long time on this matter, and we should probably spend even more time, so that we got the final recommendation from the Council of State. This would give us a better basis for making a decision in this case. On 22 October we had a hearing, and the media has also shown great interest in this matter. Norway has had a total ban on lead hail since 2005. It has been prohibited to produce, import, export, market and use lead hail. The Committee has received various reports, including: by the Directorate for the Environment and the Veterinary Institute. In its report, the Environment Directorate points out that new research into the damage effects of lead confirms previous knowledge and reveals new concerns about health consequences. Reducing the regulatory burden would increase the risk of harm to health, according to the Directorate-General for the Environment's report of 3 June 2014. The World Health Organization states that there is no safe lower limit where one can say that lead in the blood is not harmful. Lead contamination can lead to high blood pressure, concentration and memory loss, mental disorders and cancer. Recent studies show that predators such as hawks, eagles and owls are exposed to poisoning through consumption of prey animals containing lead hail. Both the Institute of Public Health and the Veterinary Institute warn against lifting the ban on lead hail. Other organisations, such as the Norwegian Hunters’ and Fishers’ Associations, point out that lead hail is the most effective ammunition, and that steel ammunition, which is the most recent option, increases the risk of damage shooting and thus increases the potential for suffering in the prey animals. Blyhagl ensures a more animal-ethical hunt, says Norway's Hunter and Fisher Federation. The Norwegian Institute of Natural Research concludes with the Committee that we do not know enough to lift the lead hail ban and that we need more research in Norway. As rapporteur, I would like to thank you for your input and for your cooperation with the proposers and others in this matter. There are a number of comments in this setting, and I expect that the different parties will explain their views in a good way. I will therefore concentrate on the Christian People's Party's vision in the rest of the article. In the light of the technical advice given in reports, letters and consultations and the need for more time from the Council of State in this matter, it is clear to the Christian People's Party that we will not now open up the use of lead hail in Norwegian nature. We do not want to waste people’s health – postponing children, pregnant women and women of childbearing potential for lead exposure, where low exposures can have effects. For the Christian People's Party, it is essential to clarify further the consequences of more lead entering the food chain, that we gain reassuring knowledge about the danger of potential adverse health effects for animals and humans. It is a paradox when lead is included in the national list of substances harmful to health and the environment to be reduced and stopped by 2020 that we are now going to reintroduce and allow lead hail outside wetlands and shooting areas. An embellishment of the prohibition will, of course, help to make this goal more difficult to achieve. We're going in the opposite direction, so we're going backwards into the future, and it's only five years until 2020. I end by promoting the proposals of the Christian People's Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e2134	The case concerns both Document 8:87 S for 2013–2014 on an action plan for a non-toxic day-to-day life and Document 8:104 S for 2013–2014, which is a proposal from SV to ban environmental pollutants. I would like to thank the Committee for a good job. There has also been a good hearing just before Christmas, 16 December, where very many organisations and stakeholders gave us important moments in dealing with these Document 8 proposals. It is also very nice to note that the Committee reached an agreement on an action plan for a non-toxic day of life, and that it will be adopted here today in the United Kingdom. A number of the products consumed daily by Norwegian consumers have a high level of pollutants that have both health and environmental effects, and there is a broad consensus that the use of pollutants should be reduced. Toxic and endocrine disrupting substances can cause harmful effects separately, but they may be particularly harmful when combined with each other. Norway has committed itself to limiting the spread of environmental pollutants through the generation target and has stated that emissions and use of substances posing a serious threat to health and the environment shall be continuously reduced with the aim of halting emissions by 2020. It is very positive that the Committee has agreed to ask the government to draw up an action plan for a non-toxic everyday life. It is also important that the action plan should include action against environmental pollutants both nationally and internationally, measures for increased supervision and control, consumer information and, in particular, industrial dialogue. It is an important step forward for Norway that we can now have such an action plan that both Sweden and Denmark already have, and this should lead to a long-term effort towards the goal of reducing the use of pollutants. The need for more control and supervision was raised several times during the hearing held by the Committee in this case. Many of the banned pollutants are still in constant use, and the illegal use of such pollutants has to be detected to a greater extent than at present. The Committee also agrees to call on the government to ensure that international regulation and prohibition of the most dangerous non-regulated toxins in products available in Norway are implemented as quickly as possible. In addition, the government is asked to work internationally to ensure that all products containing harmful chemicals have a content declaration showing which substances and the amount of substances contained in the products. Opinions are shared within the Committee on individual proposals and also on the extent to which an international process can relate to national measures. I'm assuming that the different fractions will explain that separately. From the centre party we are concerned that the international processes take a very long time. Seven years ago, substances with particular concern were included in the EU candidate list, and over these seven years, 22 substances have been finalised. At the same time, the list and candidate list now cover a total of 155 substances. If you don't get any progress in this work, the list will grow further, and there will be an unsustainable situation. For this reason, it is also the case that several individual countries have introduced their own regulations. Denmark has, for example: a ban on parabens in products for children under three years of age. It is often used in impregnated textiles, typical park suits and raincoats. And Sweden has for the first time appealed to the Court of Justice of the European Union for the absence of restrictions on endocrine disrupting substances within the time limit set. It is precisely one of the main problems of international work, that the insidiousness seen in the EU leads to the cessation of the phase-out of dangerous substances. Both the Danish Government and the European Parliament's Committee on Justice support Sweden in this matter, and it is also important to look at what Norway is doing in this way. International regulation provides good protection against pollutants, but we cannot sit still and wait for EU regulations to come into place. The central party believes that when several countries are in charge of their own legislation and dare to go ahead, Norway must also have the courage to do so. In sum, it will also be able to press forward stronger and faster international regulation of pollutants. Voluntary labelling schemes also play a central role in reducing the amount of environmental toxins, and the discussion of the space for action of national regulation does not least lead to the very importance of labelling schemes. In this context, the Swan Label is an important issue. We look forward to adopting the issue of an action plan here today, and I will also be allowed to address the proposals No. 3 and 4 from the Central Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Green Party in the Environment.
