ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e2188	Environmental toxins are increasingly used in products we use and surround ourselves with every single day. It is demanding for consumers to deal with, not least because we rarely know what the products we buy contain. Even I had a visit to the Norwegian Institute of Air Research in my own daughter's bedroom. For one day, the air was tested. The result was discouraging. More than 300 different chemical compounds were discovered, many of which were carcinogenic, endocrine disrupting and fertility reducing. How all these drugs work together, we don't know too much about. Many people talk about a cocktail effect of the different pollutants we're surrounding. At my house there was probably plastic toys, wall paint and electronics that went out, but you can't be sure. We also know that clothing and packaging can contain large amounts of environmental toxins. It's a cross of thought that we're encouraged to wash new clothes twice before we put them into service. However, although environmental exposure to the body is reduced by this, environmental pollutants end up in nature. Once they're introduced into a product, it's almost impossible to avoid human beings and nature being exposed somehow. Also the air of the staging representative Helga Pedersen and in a nursery on Tøen was tested by the Norwegian Institute for Air Research. Although there were variations in which drugs were found, the extent was the same as in my home. Something has to be done, and I am very pleased that the government has announced that it will draw up an action plan for a non-toxic day of work, and that the Stortinge agrees today on just such an invitation to the government by decision-making. The action plan must be concrete and committed and contribute to better consumer information, more knowledge and stronger supervision and control than at present. Voluntary labelling schemes are an important tool that helps us as consumers to make good choices in everyday life. The Swan label is for example: well incorporated. I believe that the government in its new strategy needs to assess how labelling schemes can be used to stimulate more – both businesses, the public sector and consumers – to opt out of products containing pollutants. First of all, however, we have to work internationally. The few products we buy in Norway are produced in Norway. If we are to succeed in reducing the number of environmental toxins in products, we must therefore work internationally, especially against the EU. Norway is an integral part of the EU internal market. It is therefore limited in our ability to adopt national bans on pollutants. For example, ESA has: notified that they consider bringing an action against Norway in the EFTA Court, as a result of the introduction in 2013 of a national prohibition on the content of perfluorooctanoic acid, PFOA. We will address the same issue if we try to introduce strange rules on content declarations. It is therefore essential that the government give very high priority to international work in the announced strategy. Fortunately, we see that the EU is working a lot on this issue. A list of harmful pollutants was drawn up by the Environment Directorate in 2007. Most of these substances are currently either regulated in the EU/EEA or there are proposals for such regulation. Today, only two to three of the 21 substances identified by the Environment Directorate in 2007 are not regulated, proposed or assessed as not posing a significant problem. This is mainly due to the introduction of the EU Chemicals Directive REACH in Norway in 2008 and demonstrates the value of having a common regulatory framework in this area. Once the EU has decided, a market of 500 million inhabitants will have a significant impact, not only on domestic production in the EU, but on output in countries that want to sell their goods to the EU. While we must work for international regulations and bans to limit the number of new pollutants in the products we surround, we must help clean up old sins. Many fjords and ports in Norway are heavily contaminated by former industrial activities. An example of a cleared port area is Oslo Havn. The results are good. The fjord is cleaner than in decades, and the city's inhabitants can again swim and fish in the fjord without worrying about health. Several such projects are under way, including: in Bodø, but there are still many who have not developed good plans and put in place cooperation between the owners of the ports, municipalities and the state, on cleaning up. The government has increased the allocation to 72 million. kr – for the work on clearing up fjords and ports in 2015, which provides a starting point for launching projects in several places in the country.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e2224	Plays that damage the ability to have children, food papers that leave fluorine on the brown cheese, and clothes that can cause cancer, disrupt hormones and cause diabetes and behavioral disorders – that is what we are talking about today. This is not the first time the theme has been put on the agenda. In the past, the Consumer Council has provided research pointing to the risk of use of endocrine disrupting substances in cosmetics. At that time, the Consumer Council launched an iPhone programme to help consumers choose products without endocrine disrupting substances. The Christian People's Party called for an action plan for a non-toxic everyday life. In this context, there has been particular concern about parabens, which have been used for years to prevent fungi and bacteria in jars and tubes. Researchers have suspected that parabens can lead to poor sperm quality and testicular cancer in men. They also put parabens in conjunction with the increase in breast cancer. Today we have guidelines on the concentration of these substances in the products, but no information that the combination of several products with these substances can produce a endocrine disrupting effect, a so-called cocktail effect. The cocktail effect means that substances that are harmless in small amounts may nevertheless have endocrine disrupting effects when combined with other substances. There is a need to spend more resources on control and supervision. We also know too little about the damage the poisons can cause. Therefore, more documentation and research is also needed. In a survey carried out by the Consumer Council before Christmas, nine out of ten replied that they wanted stricter regulation of harmful chemicals in toys and other products. According to Our Country, both Sweden and Denmark have action plans for a more toxic everyday life. This has led – as several have mentioned earlier – to the fact that the Swedish authorities have fivefold the supervision. Swedish authorities have found an illegal number of dangerous chemicals in 10 pst. of the products examined. Toys and electronics are those product groups where dangerous chemicals are most often present. In its annual evaluation, the Swedish authorities note that many companies are too poorly aware of the rules applicable to toxic substances. I am pleased that the Storting is today gathering to ask ‘the government to draw up an action plan for a non-toxic day-to-day life’, a plan that includes ‘measures against environmental pollutants both nationally and internationally, measures for increased supervision and control, consumer information and industry dialogue’. A strong international pressure is very important to approach the goal of a non-toxic everyday life. That is why I am pleased that the Stortinget ‘is asking the government to work for the fastest possible international regulation and prohibition of the most dangerous non-regulated toxins in products available in Norway, based on updated technical recommendations from the Environment Directorate’. In addition, the government is asking ‘to work internationally for all products containing harmful chemicals to have a content declaration that shows which substances and quantities of these products contain’ is also very good. Finally, I must say that it is a pity that we do not get a majority to include the collection of unused medicines as part of the action plan for a non-toxic everyday life. Pharmacies are currently receiving unused medicinal products. To what extent drugs are delivered back there, we do not really know anything about it. Trials carried out in some municipalities, where one has included medicines in the municipal waste system, have been a success. I'm afraid the reality today is that very much is being washed down at home. In general, I believe that in order to succeed in the collection of environmentally hostile waste, the systems must be as easily accessible as possible. That should also be the case here.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e2482	I will present the vote on behalf of the President-in-Office. In this case, there is a shared committee with a large majority and a minority. The Committee wishes to refer to the two-part objective resulting from the robberies agreement. The management of predators is based on a policy whereby the objective of ensuring the survival of all the major predatory species in Norwegian nature must be combined with an administration which, in total, helps to mitigate conflicts and counter insecurity within the two-part objective. The agreement also states that all predator management should be based on scientific and experience-based knowledge, and emphasis should be placed on regional governance, respect for property rights and the quality of life of individuals and communities. The Committee is of the opinion that private ownership is important in order to ensure good management of natural resources and that private owners of land have a particular interest in operating their property in a long-term perspective. Then we get to where we separate layers. The majority of the Committee refers to the reply letter from Tine Sundtoft, Minister for Climate and Environment. The compensation, compensation and subsidy schemes to which the grazing industry has access are set out here. This is compensation for grazing animals taken from predatory game subsidies for preventive measures against predatory game damage grants for operational conversion due to predatory game compensation for grazing restrictions The majority of the Committee considers that there are currently good financial arrangements for owners of grazing animals affected by predatory game. Good arrangements are important to ensure a viable grazing industry. The majority in the Committee refers to proposal 8:1 S for 2014-2015, where one promotes a proposal to amend the provision of compensation in Section 19 of the Natural Diversity Act, so that it is equivalent to Section 50 of the Act, which deals with compensation in protected areas. The majority would point out that § 50 is a derogation from the rules otherwise applicable to the liability of the State and that there are a number of financial arrangements for animal owners within and outside predatory areas. The majority will therefore not recommend the proposal set out in Document 8:1 S.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e2538	The proposal asks that the government amends the provision of compensation in Section 19 of the Nature Diversity Act, so that it becomes equivalent to Section 50 of the same law. It would have fully compensated livestock keepers in the breeding area of predators for their economic losses in grazing, loss of feeding grounds if the livestock population had to be reported or when extensive preventive measures are required. It may be carried out by replacing the economic loss of the keeper of the livestock by having to indicate the holding, or alternatively, that any additional costs incurred by continuing the holding will be borne. It is now the case that, in order to create acceptance and understanding of the establishment of predatory wildlife populations and at the same time to reduce the level of conflict, it will be essential for a person to continue the principle that the individual citizen should not suffer financial loss and have to give up its nutritional basis without replacing it. In the judgment of the Supreme Court, cited in Norsk Retstidende 2006, page 1105, the Rendals judgment, Section 34 states: ‘Our predatory policy is based on the belief that the individual citizen shall not be charged with the cost of damage suffered or harm prevention measures.’ In other words, it is the big community that must bear the economic consequences of the country's predatory policy, not the individual livestock keeper. However, today’s situation is that in the breeding area of predators, keepers are only allowed to replace the loss of the animal’s value with direct consequential damage. That is what is contained in Section 19 of the Nature Diversity Act and its related regulations. It contrasts with livestock farmers with grazing rights in areas reserved for national parks, landscape areas, nature reserves or other. There's another replacement regime. Section 50 of the Nature Diversity Act operates and that provision states that livestock keepers are compensated for their financial loss «when a protection entails a difficulty in the current use». Farmers with grazing rights in protected areas thus enjoy a stronger protection of replacement law than similar keepers with grazing rights in the breeding area for predators. It is not suitable to arouse confidence. The desire of the centre party is that the two groups should be equated in principle and in law. There is no need to distinguish between the replacement protection of grazing or ownership keepers in the breeding area for predators and herds keepers with grazing and ownership rights in nature conservation areas. In many cases, the problem of predators has a greater economic impact on landowners and rightholders in the breeding area of predators than on landowners with protection under the Nature Diversity Act § 50. Farmers with rights of gravitation and ownership in the breeding area of predators must withstand the limitations of availability due to the requirements and needs of the large community, and they can withstand restrictions which are at least similar to those of the keepers affected by the protection provided for in Section 50. The parties therefore consider that the compensation regime is incompatible with the principles of protection of private property and with the principles of compensation that were used to provide comparable restrictions on the availability of those restricted by nature protection and protected areas. We also believe that the replacement regime is not compatible with the principles laid down in the Rendals judgment. We're all going to show you to and use the rough game deal. In the prey agreement, grazing is also a positive thing: «Animals on the estuary have many positive qualities and it is important to ensure the viability of agricultural activities in areas of wild game.» But what's about to happen is you can no longer do it in the breeding grounds for predators. The parties to the settlement continue to point to the firmness of the two-part objective, but it is soon only as a mouthhole to count in terms of grazing and grazing rights. Then we also determine in the robberies agreement: ‘It is assumed that predator management is carried out in such a way that no grazing is required under the Animal Welfare Act [[...]] We're not meeting the two-part goal today. We don't meet the two-part objective when we actually see that a number of areas become inaccessible for grazing. Therefore, it is sad that some are not willing to enter into the discussion and neither deal with nor acknowledge the issue contained in the proposal from the Centre Party. With that, I take up proposal No. 1 in the settings.

Such a scheme will base the fact that predators in nature are one of the framework conditions for sheep farming, and it will provide a stronger incentive for solutions. At the same time, no matter what compensation scheme we choose, it is also necessary to limit as much uncertainty as possible as to the basis for compensation payments. Last autumn, a study by the Norwegian Institute for Natural Research concluded that compensation for loss of sheep is paid to gloupe for considerably more sheep than gloapa actually takes. This gives rise to an already significant uncertainty about the precision of predatory damages, including in the case of other predatory species. The Greens have therefore also made proposals today to follow up the Gaupe study of the Norwegian Institute for Natural Research last year with similar studies on the loss of sheep for all species of farmed game. I take this up with me the two proposals that the Greens have promoted in this position.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e2747	There is broad consensus in the Committee that the main principles of Norwegian climate policy for 2020 will be based on the agreement of the 2012 climate agreement. It provides predictability for most people and decision-makers in industry, industry and the public sector. There is consensus in the Committee that we need to pursue an ambitious national climate policy that leads to a long-term transition to a low-carbon society by 2050. As one of the richest countries in the world, Norway has a unique opportunity to show that it is possible to combine high living standards with low emissions. At the same time, an ambitious national policy must be sensible in a global context. The overall objective is to reduce total global greenhouse gas emissions. This implies taking into account the consequences of the quota system, the risk of carbon leakage and the competitiveness of industry when designing policies. This provides guidance for the use of the instrument to reduce national emissions towards both 2020, 2030 and 2050. It is gratifying that an overall committee is behind this view. It is necessary to strengthen climate reconciliation through new measures if we are to manage to reduce emissions in Norway. The government and the cooperation parties have already implemented a great many measures that will reduce emissions. In the transport sector, a number of measures have been taken to speed up the transition. In 2015, the turnover of biofuels is increased from 3,5 pst. to 5.5 pst. This measure alone will reduce emissions by a minimum of 130 000 tonnes of CO2. With effect from 1 July, the biodiesel levy will be eliminated, the low-mixed bioethanol levy will be halved and a full user charge for natural gas and LPG will be introduced. The last step will make biogas competitive. The tax advantages for electric cars continue in 2015, and in addition, chargeable hybrids have become cheaper. All these measures will help to accelerate the transition of the transport sector. At the same time, there is a strong focus on rail and public transport, and the government has promoted new guidelines for environmentally friendly local development. In sum, living without a car in everyday life will be simpler and more efficient. In the construction sector, the government has extended and extended the right-holders’ aid scheme for an additional measure under Enova. Several measures are supported and the total amount of aid has increased. This strengthens the incentives to replace fossil oil firing with environmentally friendly alternatives. As of 2020, there has been a ban on fossil fuel oil firing in households and on the baseload of other buildings. Renewable energy is an important part of the solution for achieving the low-carbon society. Still almost 40 pst. of Norway’s energy use fossil despite the renewableity of electricity production. Norway has a significant potential for developing more renewable energy. This energy should mainly be used to replace fossil energy where possible and appropriate, to facilitate more environmentally friendly industrial production and energy intensive activities in Norway and to allow more power exchanges with our neighbours on the continent and in the United Kingdom. This will have both positive environmental impacts and contribute to the creation of new green jobs and green value creation in Norway. The government has taken several steps to realise our renewable potential. The point of entry for the land interest tax has been raised. Better depreciation rules for wind power have been notified and an extension of the electricity licence market to 2021 has been consulted. In addition, two new inter-national links with Germany and the United Kingdom have been granted a concession. Half of Norwegian emissions are covered by the EU ETS. In this sector, emissions will be reduced by 43 pst. in 2030 compared to 2005, the EEA territory as a whole. It is an effective and effective instrument that, at the same time, provides Norway with flexibility to invest in environmentally friendly industrial production in the years to come. At the same time, Norway must have ambitions to produce as cleanly as possible in the quota sector. New technologies are essential to achieve this. In this context, it is worth noting that the government has decided to strengthen the Climate, Renewable Energy and Energy Conversion Fund by 12.75 billion. kr in addition to the agreement in the climate agreement. At the same time, the Norwegian eco-technology system has increased to 330 million. kr in 2015. In total, this will enable far more enterprises to realise good environmental projects, build more pilots and develop and implement new technologies that reduce emissions and energy use. There is little doubt that government policy represents a shift in climate policy. Together with our cooperation parties, we have already delivered concrete and ambitious new measures after a year and a half in the government that will contribute to lower emissions and new green jobs in Norway. I am pleased that the measures have broad support, and that the Stortinget has been able to gather on five new measures through the consideration of this proposal for a representative. I therefore recommend that the vote be taken.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e3066	As the rapporteur, I would like to thank the rapporteurs Per Olaf Lundtägen and Marit Arnstad for putting an important issue on the agenda and the other constructive cooperation committee. Everyone has striven to ensure solutions that give a good start to the natural knowledge promise that needs to come. The government parties have shown flexibility, so that we now stand together to ensure an ecological base map for Norway. Let me draw out two things. We who have followed the environmental debate for a while know that the sharing of responsibilities between the Art Database and the Environment Directorate is demanding. With this setting, the Committee clearly determines the new role of the Species Database in the development of the organic base map for Norway. A unanimous committee writes: «The species database shall be responsible for the development of computer systems for the practical nature-type mapping of fields, the storage of nature-type data, the quality assurance of these data and the provision of the data to all users.» This means that professional knowledge of Norwegian species and natural habitat types is gathered in one place and that the Art Database is nabbed into a factual system to which all actors in all sectors must relate. At the same time, the Environment DG will continue to play its central role in using knowledge in practical management. A broad majority of the Committee shall require that the Executive Board: ‘recommends work on defining and ordering the identification of the valuable natural habitat types to be surveyed. The Environment Directorate shall also develop criteria for the valuation of nature and provide guidance on the management and care of nature. The Environment Directorate shall also continue the Nature Base, where demarcation of management areas based on the baseline data from nature type mapping is an element». We assume that the Minister for Climate and Environment quickly implements this clear division of labour in practice. It is essential to maximise the 25 million that the Storting has allocated to a knowledge gap for nature. And I hope that the Minister for Climate Change and the Environment can confirm that this work is being undertaken quickly. Secondly, I am pleased that a broad majority in the Committee sees the urgent need to establish an ecological base map for Norway while we «provides that all existing natural base data and administrative councils are maintained and included in the land management knowledge base and are taken into account in land management». The consideration of the Committee requires that Norwegian nature be managed on a knowledge-based basis and that the best decisions and the most up-to-date knowledge be taken into account. This is a fundamental view that obliges and that both government and opposition should notice. This applies not only to parties, but above all to determining critical issues in the management plan for the sea areas, in decisions on marine landfills for mining waste or on new water power developments. We need value-neutral knowledge to know what and where the ice edge is and whether mining waste threatens the blulange spawning grounds. When we say that we want to base the objective, value-neutral facts of the Art Database on natural management, we also have to do that, for example. where the same Species database establishes that the species of wolf is critically threatened in Norway. In future sustainable Norway, which all seem to agree that we will develop – there is only a little disagreement about whether we want to do it now or another time – nature must necessarily have greater significance and value than it has today. A society in balance with the environment and the future needs to be based much more on resources that reproduce themselves – that is, on nature. Therefore, the need for more knowledge of Norwegian nature extends far beyond what is needed to ensure traditional nature protection. Today's good decision on a more knowledge-based nature management must therefore be a start, and the 25 million we now spend this year have to be multiplied many times to cover the natural mapping that we're going to need for the future. The basis for comparison is the billions we invest annually in petroleum screening. It is investment in natural knowledge in such a magnitude that we must think as we further develop the knowledge base for a sustainable future.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e3112	The natural diversity is the core wall on which people build society and economy. Robust ecosystems with a wide variety of species and genes ensure access to food, clean water and the natural resources we use to build houses and provide energy. They provide experiences and quality of life and are an important part of the culture of many different societies. In Norway, we are equipped with a varied nature and a variety of forms of life it is important to take care of. The ecosystem of the world's diversity of species provides countless and essential services. It is therefore essential that we manage biodiversity in a sustainable way. Human beings have a fundamental responsibility to manage nature. We have a duty to preserve our basis of life in such a way that we hand over the globe to our descendants in at least as good a condition as when we took it. Nature must therefore be managed with respect, on its own merits. Nature's diversity has been developed naturally over a long period of time and through the influence of civilization. It is an important task to take care of the diversity of nature for future generations. In the opinion of the Right, this is best done through a combination of protection and sustainable use. Art diversity has changed throughout earth's history, species have disappeared, changed, and new ones have emerged. This is a natural part of evolution and the way of life. This is why, among other things, it disappeared. The dinosaurs 65 million years ago, and a number of other species have disappeared as a result of their failure to adapt to changing living conditions on earth. This development has always occurred and will continue. What is serious is the extinction of species that are not part of the natural evolution and life-form of the earth, but that are in addition as a result of human activity, and are in progress at a much faster pace. This extinction of species is what we have to stop. However, although there are different perceptions of the speed with which species are eradicated, it is important that measures are put in place to preserve species diversity. Through the Convention on Biological Diversity, the world community has agreed to halt the eradication of species, and under this Convention, the country of the world of Nagoia agreed in 2010 on a strategic plan to achieve this objective by 2020. The Aichi targets are a plan consisting of 20 targets. The Convention is important, but the most important thing is how to follow it up at home in order to achieve its objectives. Many of the targets will be demanding in Norway, and they face a number of dilemmas and potential conflicts. An important tool to address natural diversity is the Nature Diversity Act, adopted by the Stortinget in spring 2009. It provides a common framework, principles and rules for the sustainable use and protection of natural diversity. An important prerequisite for the proper functioning of the Nature Diversity Act is that we have sufficient knowledge of different species, habitat types and ecosystems. The Government and the Cooperation Parties have launched the Knowledge Promise. In 2015, it's worth 25 million. kr for the mapping of endangered species and natural habitats. It's a good start. Increased knowledge is essential in order to pursue an environmental policy based on administrative responsibility. At the same time, increased knowledge will make it easier to reconcile nature's needs with the needs of modern civilization. In the future, too, we will need new roads, lines, power transmissions and more buildings and dwellings. A strong knowledge base will ensure more efficient and less contentious planning and development processes. The socio-economic gain of increased knowledge and increased monitoring will be significant. The Government has announced that it will present a new Action Plan on Natural Diversity in 2015 and that the Action Plan will include proposals for actions that will improve data base, data quality, databases and, in particular, data sharing. A high priority must be given to the acquisition of new concrete knowledge on the occurrence of valuable natural habitats. Today, the species database plays a very important and central role in providing objective assessments of red lists and blacklisting of species and habitat types. It is natural that the Species database plays a central role in the development of computer systems for the practical nature-type mapping, storage of nature-type data, quality assurance and making available. Let me end by saying that it is gratifying that there is such a broad consensus in the Stortinget about investing in increased knowledge of Norwegian nature. It is important to rust Norway for the future.

Why do we find such a special alliance, must we say, together? Well, all experience suggests that when you have knowledge of natural values at the beginning of a planning process, it will often be possible to find alternatives and adaptations that reduce points of conflict or even eliminate them altogether – faster decisions in construction processes and less of threatening nature delay and cost the construction of roads and dwellings. At the same time, the speed of planning processes will increase because good mapping of natural values can take time. They are often seasonal and may require some planning. ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e3163	Why is there a need for a promise of knowledge for natural diversity now? The background is that Norway is one of the most diverse countries in Europe — from north to south, from east to west, from sea to high mountain. We are a rare country, with great variety and thus great species wealth. Exploration of the diversity of species and natural habitats was central to research and teaching at Norwegian universities and higher education institutions until the 1980s. Since then, internationalisation and research and education have completely changed the criteria for eligible research. Research with the main purpose of describing natural variation, species and ecosystems is not funded by the Research Council in 2015. Professionalism is under pressure, and there is a link between the reduction of professional environments and the weakening of protection on a scientific basis. And further, education: The weakening of natural diversity research at universities has led to the dwindling of research-based education. Field education has been greatly reduced, particularly in lower-level studies. And new candidates have much weaker knowledge of species, habitat types and mapping methods today than they did 30 years ago. Paradoxically, the demand for candidates with such broad knowledge has increased sharply. And the third: long-term. Nature is complex, deep natural diversity requires a diverse background in biology, geology, geography, chemistry and history – and many years of field work. Long-termity is a prerequisite for building up and maintaining such knowledge in individual and professional environments, and competence must be transferred between the scientific generations in order not to be lost. How should responsibility for different tasks within the nature diversity field be allocated? The background is that several hundred million dollars have been spent on the mapping of natural habitats and landscapes in Norway. Large parts of this mapping do not meet reasonable precision, value neutrality and reprobability requirements. The monitoring results must be quality assurance and must be party neutral in order to meet society’s needs for knowledge of the prevalence and state of natural habitat types and for monitoring changes. Knowledge-based natural management requires a clear distinction between three responsibilities. Paragraph 1 is overall professional responsibility – e.g. for the development of nature-type schemes, the mapping methods and the maintenance of species denominations, the certification of surveyors, the training and the continuation of training. Most of these tasks are historically part of the social mission of the university museums. The universities and their free role today prevent the Ministry of Knowledge from instructing universities to continue this responsibility. Because education has to be research-based, the Center Party believes that these tasks still have to be the responsibility of the University Museums, here the Ministry of Knowledge and the Ministry of Climate and Environment need to find a solution together. For the Centre Party, the obvious solution is a centre of natural diversity knowledge at the museums based on the good experience of the National Biosystematics Research School. And responsibility for maintaining national competence among different species groups and for nature-type variation needs to be allocated to different environments and long-term funding ensured. Point 2: Who will then be responsible for storing and making available value-neutral nature type data? This is where the Art Database is central. As a party-neutral knowledge institution, the Art Database should also be given a similar responsibility for nature-type information. Finally, responsibility for administrative assessments, including valuations, prioritisation of management measures, has to be assigned to each sectoral authority. So this is about: to have clear distinctions between the professional responsibility of the university museums to make knowledge available – the Art Database administrative assessments, for which each department is responsible Finally, I would like to address the proposal of the Centre Party, the proposals No. 1 and 2.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e3386	The sea areas are of great importance to Norway. The sea and the coast have shaped the development of Norwegian society and shaped a lot of our identity. The sea holds great environmental values and resources, both what we've known for a long time, and things that we don't yet have knowledge of. Norwegian trade and economy are strongly linked to the sea, from maritime transport and shipbuilding to fish and aquaculture, oil and gas. Norway shall be a leading country in the overall and ecosystem-based management of marine areas. It is an objective to ensure a holistic ecosystem-based management that takes care of natural diversity and provides a basis for the sustainable use of resources. Internationally, Norway has a leading role to play in developing knowledge and management of the environment and resources in the sea and continental shelf. The management plans have gained significant international recognition. A number of countries have now established a similar management of marine areas, including The EU through the Marine Strategy Directive. Climate change and increased activity in marine areas are constantly creating new challenges. The port of Norway has an interest in maintaining and further developing the role of a responsible manager of the sea. In St.meld. No 12 for 2001–02, Clean and rich seas, the so-called Marine Environment Communication, promoted a more ecosystem-based and cross-sectoral marine environmental policy with comprehensive management plans as a tool. In the marine environment communication, it appeared, among other things: at: ‘The overall management plans for the sea areas will, on the basis of the sectoral impact studies, provide the basis for the establishment of protection areas and provide general guidelines for activities in the sea areas.’;» The situation was then characterised by fragmented management and lack of an overall knowledge base. Whole management plans have now been drawn up for all Norwegian sea areas. These are all based on common professional bases developed across sectors. The aim of the management plans is to facilitate value creation through the sustainable use of resources and ecosystem services in marine areas while maintaining the structure, functioning, productivity and natural diversity of ecosystems. The management plans are therefore a tool for both the organisation of value creation and the maintenance of environmental values in marine areas. The management plans contribute to the transparency of the overall framework, coordination and priorities in the management of marine areas. With this in mind, Norway has a policy tool for overall maritime governance and for the legitimacy of decisions on the balance between business interests and environmental concerns in our seas. As you know, the management plans have received broad support in Stortinget. The interpellence of the representative of Aasland provides an opportunity to clarify that the work of the management plan takes place in two stages. The first stage is the technical basic work, while the second stage is the management plans themselves for the individual sea areas. It is in the management plans themselves that the government presents its overall policy to balance environmental concerns and the sustainable use of marine resources. Let me state immediately that the government will continue the management plans for the sea areas as political tools. Due to the rapid changes taking place in the Barents Sea, related to climate change and the melting of the sea, the government during this major period will ensure the start of work on a technical basis for a full revision of the Barents Sea and off-Lofoten seas management plan. The revised management plan will be presented in a Communication to the Stortinget in 2020. The size has provided for similar revisions to the Norwegian Sea Management Plan in 2025 and to the North Sea and Skagerrak management plan in 2030. An important intervention in the management plans is the identification of some areas such as particularly valuable and vulnerable areas. These are areas which, based on scientific assessments, have a significant impact on biological production and/or biodiversity in marine areas, even outside the areas themselves. These areas have a high density of environmental values such as fish, seabird, etc. and are vulnerable to various human influences. Actions in the management plans have chosen to show particular care in the particularly valuable and vulnerable areas. Such a particularly valuable and vulnerable area is the edge of the ice. When examining the management plan for the Barents Sea and the Lofoten in 2011, the Stortinget concluded that no petroleum activities were to be started at the ice edge of the previous grossing-up period. This has continued in the government's political platform based on the cooperation agreement with the Christian People's Party and the Left for the current period. At the same time, Stortinget has provided that this does not preclude petroleum activities throughout the Barent Sea in the south-east. The edge of the ice has moved north in recent years. As most have noted, during the spring of 2015, the government will provide the Stortinget with an update of the Barents Sea and Lofoten management plan with an up-to-date calculation of the ice edge. The edge of the management plan is defined, on a professional basis, back to 2003, and has served as the basis for indicating the particularly valuable and vulnerable area at the edge of the Barents Sea and Lofoten management plan in 2006 and 2011. This is now being updated with new ice data from satellite observations. A follow-up system has been established under the management plans with regular monitoring, assessment and reporting of the state of the environment and other relevant conditions. This is done through the cross-sectoral advisory groups the Monitoring Group and the Professional Forum. Professional work is a continuous process. The monitoring group consists of relevant departments and research institutions and is managed by the Institute for the Exploration of the Sea. The mandate of the Group is laid down by the ministries concerned. The monitoring team shall contribute, according to its mandate, to the proper coordination of ongoing monitoring and to the compilation and evaluation of relevant monitoring results. The monitoring team shall disseminate the results of the team’s professional work and report regularly on developments in the state of the environment in each of the three Norwegian seas, inter alia. on the environmental status.no website. In addition to the Monitoring Group, a Professional Forum for the Integrated Ecosystem-based Management of Norwegian Seas, chaired by the Environment Directorate and composed of departments and research institutions under the departments concerned, has been established. Professional forums work on the basis of a common mandate established by all the ministries concerned. The Group shall develop the overall technical basis for updates and revisions of the management plans, including: describe changes and trends in the state of the environment as well as the activity of industries, current and future land use, impacts on the environment as well as the value creation of marine industries. The reports are presented publicly and are subject to consultation, so that organisations and the public also have the opportunity to comment on the professional basis and any other matters they consider should be taken into account when the government is to submit a management plan. In this way, it is a specific feature of the management plans that the professional base work is carried out in full across the underlying departments and relevant research institutions. When updating or revising the management plans, a common technical basis is normally compiled from the Professional Forum after ordering from the ministries and in line with the mandate of the Professional Forum. This is the central basis for the subsequent policy process of updating or revising the management plan itself. When updating the Barents Sea and Lofoten management plan with an up-to-date calculation of the ice edge, the appointment of the technical basis is limited to this specific theme, as this is the central issue to be submitted to the Stortinget. The updates have been carried out so far for an approximate five-year cycle, while more thorough revisions have been made after about five years. every 15 years. The professional work of the management plans, in both the Monitoring Group and the Professional Forum, means that knowledge of the sea areas is kept up to date. I would therefore argue that Norwegian overall maritime management is always based on an up-to-date knowledge base. Finally, let me point out that the Stortinget has agreed that Norway should ‘be a priority country in terms of global and ecosystem-based management of marine areas’ in the treatment of the Northern Region Communication in 2011.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e3487	As stated by the Council of State, the Bondevik II Government provided the basis for the work on the overall management plan for both the Barents Sea and other Norwegian sea areas through St. meld. No 12 for 2001-2002, clean and rich seas. Sustainable development has always been a key objective of this work. The management plans are important because they establish conditions for fishing, shipping and petroleum activities in the context of sustainable development. There is a nine-time and thorough work behind a management plan. In 2009, the Norwegian Government presented its management plan for the Norwegian Sea. The work on this had been going on since 2007. The planning process started in March 2007 with factual descriptions, a social description and a larger spatial and environmental resource description, including valuable areas. Work was also started to identify indicators for monitoring. These formed the basis for further work on the consequences. There were 19 different peer reviews with an examination of the consequences. Four main investigations were carried out. New hearings followed and new reports. In 2008, a consultation conference was held in Ålesund. With this conference, and the summary of the hearing opinions subsequently, the technical work was completed. Then the politicians took over. The Norwegian Sea has a high biological production and holds a large amount of organisms, which form the basis for the rich fish, seabird and mammal populations. The marine environment is an open ecosystem and, like all natural ecosystems, it is constantly changing. Man is part of the great natural processes, and we affect them through our activities. When the impact becomes too great, ecosystems will be damaged. The answer to these challenges is to operate ecosystem-based management. This includes, inter alia: that we need to see all the different activities in the Norwegian Sea as a whole and that there are ecosystems that provide the framework for how much influence we can allow. To that it is absolutely essential with knowledge. The management plans are based on knowledge of the consequences of activities that may affect the state of the environment, the resource base, social conditions and the ability to engage in other activities. This applies in particular to petroleum activities, fishing and shipping. Management plans are therefore far more important than whether or not to operate petroleum, as the Council of State's response to the written question from the representative Hansson may seem. I agree with the inter-pellant on the need for management plans to be rolled and updated regularly. The climate change we are witnessing today is happening at great speed. Therefore, we need up-to-date knowledge to both know what the situation is, what action needs to be taken, and whether the measures we have already taken are working. Lack of knowledge makes the assessments of climate change impacts and human activity uncertain in the future. A combination of monitoring, environmental monitoring and research is necessary for a better understanding of the patterns we find and for assessing the vulnerability of species and areas and the stability of ecosystems. Climate change and overfishing can bring about major changes in the species composition and dynamics of ecosystems. In particular, we need more knowledge of the resilience of marine areas to both natural and man-made influences. We also need to know more about processes at the ice edge, at the polar front and about other particularly valuable and vulnerable areas.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e3591	I would like to repeat one thing I said in my first post, namely that Norway should ‘be a priority country in terms of global and ecosystem-based management of marine areas’, as the Stortinget joined in the treatment of the North Sea Communication in 2011. This is also the government's ambition. The management plans are a concrete expression of the ability and willingness of Norway as a coastal State to take responsibility for the major maritime areas that we manage, and this has been noted internationally. Coastal states have a clear obligation under the UN Convention on the Law of the Sea to protect the environment in their marine areas. This is closely linked to the extensive rights of coastal States under the Convention on the Law of the Sea to exploit the living marine resources and other resources on the continental shelf under their jurisdiction. In 2008, the EU adopted a directive aimed at achieving good environmental status in all European seas by 2020, the so-called Marine Strategy Directive. Good environmental status shall be ensured by the development by each Member State of marine strategies for its marine areas. The strategy includes requirements for setting environmental objectives, indicators, monitoring programmes and action programmes. In other words, the Directive largely takes into account the same approach and methodology as the Norwegian management plans. The management plans are a knowledge-based policy tool. The plans are drawing up a long-term perspective on the protection of marine ecosystems and are a key instrument for ensuring Norway’s obligations under international law to protect the environment in our marine areas. At the same time, they facilitate the use of Norway’s right under international law to make sustainable use of resources in our seas. Finally, management plans are a flexible tool by enabling policies to be changed by updating and revising the plans based on new and up-to-date knowledge within an overall framework.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-02-03.ana.ud2112e4560	Thus, today's map has been processed. But before we finish the meeting, the president would like to have a few words to say that it's actually today's last time Harald Green, Secretary at the Grand Secretariat, has his working day here at Stortinget. It is with great sadness that we thank him for his efforts on the Grand Duchy for the entire 42 years. Harald Gronn was employed by SV's high school group in 1973, where, after a few years, in 1977, he became the secretary-general. In 1990 he received the position of prime minister, and since 1995 he has been head of the General Secretariat. Few, if any, have the Storting more under the skin than Harald Green. He can put the Storting's Rules of Procedure in and out and deal with it with such authority that there are few who dare to speak up if there are any objections. [[Laughter]] He knows the long lines of this house's history, he has a very special sensitivity to existing constitutional principles, and he is completely familiar with all the rules and regulations, written as well as unwritten, that apply to the activities of a parliament. I'm sure I have the hall with me when I say that Harald Green too many of us have acted as a sort of common mentor. Either he has a seat in the secretary chair here next to me, or he has filled other roles in the Stortinget, Harald Green has stood for professional security, integrity and loyalty. He has always been willing to advise on constitutional issues and has never been unwilling to explain how committee settings should be written. Also the times that have occurred more unpredictable situations here in the hall, he has shown that he has the professional weight to handle this quickly and efficiently. It is a unique career in Stortinget that is now ending. As a member of the Secretariat of the Committee on Budgetary Reform, Harald Green was central to reforming the budgetary procedure in the 1990s. Later, by several crossroads, he has had a very decisive vote against it at all times. For one thing, Harald, you've set a standard, and I'll illustrate that through the following little story. It was a late evening, and I had been at a party meeting in Wågå. It was probably about a quarter of a.m. in the evening, and I got a phone call from a very concerned government council that had been in a lot of trouble here. Questions had been raised about taking questions after the meeting, and there were then some formal questions attached to this. I just have to say that where I sat in a bus pocket in Wågå quarter in the evening, I did not have the Rules of Procedure in a way that made it clear that I could answer that question. So, of course, I'm calling Harald Green: «Good evening, Harald Green. It's quarter past eleven. I have the following questions." It was a little quiet, so I heard that it was shaking in pages, and then I got a good answer. So I say, "Harald, can you just say this?" 'No, I have the Rules of Procedure here,' he says. ‘At eleven o’clock in the evening, do you have the Rules of Procedure?’ 'I always have the Rules of Procedure. ‘This is a standard I think should be for follow-up, also for those who come after. [[Laughter]] I would also say, Harald, that you have also set a standard for what is going on in order to hold a big meeting, which I believe is important in our digitized age. You insist that what is needed to hold a big meeting is a piece of paper, a spring pen, an ink box and a candle. I think it's worth holding on to, even in the next 200 years. It's an institution in Stortinget that we're taking bye with us, and we want you to have a long, good and active retirement life. Then you should not take the train from Larvik at 5:40 a.m. and then be in position here at Stortinget a few hours later. Actually, it should have been the case with secretaries in Stortinget who have been pardoned, that there should have been a decision that they should be allowed to stop. Then my suggestion would be that it should require a majority of the constitution. But when it's not like that, you get to let go anyway. On behalf of the entire Storting and presidency, I will be grateful for the wonderful effort you have made, and wish you all the best for an impending retirement life. Thank you very much. [[Standing applause in the Chamber]]
